Start Here

How This Book Works

"way easier than trying to find random recipes online"

— reader review

That line comes from someone who bought a different meal prep book. It names the problem this one is built for. You don't need more recipes. You need to know what to cook on Sunday, in what order, how much of it, and how long it keeps.

So this is a system first and a recipe book second.

What's inside

Part 1 · The System (Chapters 1-7). How a plate is built, how to read your Sunday Sheet, portions, storage, cost, protein, pantry. About 20 pages. You read it once.

Part 2 · The 4-Week Plan (Chapters 8-11). Each week is one Sunday Sheet, one shopping list sorted by store aisle (for 1 or 2 people) and five plates.

Part 3 · Recipes (Chapters 12-19). 60 recipes, written as parts you combine: 10 proteins, 6 bases, 6 vegetables, 12 sauces, plus 8 breakfasts, 10 one-pot freezer meals and 8 snacks. Then 48 signature plates and the Mix & Match chart.

Part 4 · Reference (Chapters 20-23). Storage chart, macro-cost-allergen index, fixes for things that go wrong, blank templates.

Every recipe card shows the same facts in the same spot: yield in grams and boxes, calories and protein per portion, allergens, days in the fridge and freezer, how to reheat, and what it goes with.

Pick your path

Path A · Follow Week 1
For you if you want zero decisions this time.
Read Chapter 2 (the sheet) and Chapter 4 (storage). That's about 15 minutes. Then go to Chapter 8, shop from the list and cook from the sheet.
You end Sunday with 10 boxes and 5 breakfasts. That's lunch and dinner Monday to Friday for one person, or lunch for two.

Path B · Build your own plates
For you if you've prepped before and know what you like.
Read Chapters 1, 3 and 6. Then go to the sauces (Chapter 15) and the Mix & Match chart (Chapter 16). Plan your week on the blank sheet in Chapter 23.

Path C · Freezer only
For you if you can't give up a Sunday right now, or you get tired of the same box by day three.
Read Chapter 4. Then go to the Freezer Block (Chapter 18): 10 one-pot meals you cook on any free evening, portion and freeze. You build a stock and take out one box at a time.

Not sure? Take Path A. Week 1 uses the parts the rest of the book builds on.

What "about 2 hours" means

The plan weeks are designed around a Sunday cooking session of about 2 hours for one person's week. That's a design target. It is not a promise.

Your first Sunday will probably run longer. You're reading as you go. Your oven may run cooler than the dial says. Shopping isn't counted, and neither is the last round of dishes.

Time your first Sunday and write the real number on your sheet. Your number is the one that matters. Write it down each week and see how it moves once the steps stop being new.

Everything you fill in or stick on the fridge is also in the printable pack (US Letter, black-and-white friendly).

Before you cook: three notes

1. This is not medical or nutrition advice. Each recipe shows calories and protein per portion, calculated from USDA data (Chapter 6). The book does not tell you how much to eat. If you have a medical condition, are pregnant, or follow a diet set by a professional, talk to a registered dietitian or your doctor before you change how you eat.

2. Allergens. Each recipe lists which of the 9 major food allergens recognized in the US it contains: milk, eggs, fish, crustacean shellfish, tree nuts, peanuts, wheat, soybeans and sesame. Brands differ. Soy sauce usually contains wheat. Many tortillas and some gochujang do too. Products can also be made on shared lines. Read the label of the package you actually buy. This book can't see it.

3. Food safety. Storage times and cooking temperatures follow USDA Food Safety and Inspection Service (FSIS) guidance. Chapter 4 has the rules and the sources. Use a thermometer. If something smells off or you're unsure how old it is, follow the FSIS rule: when in doubt, throw it out.

Your first action

Fill this in before you turn the page.

My path A · B · C
My Sunday cooking window from ______ to ______
I cook for 1 person · 2 people
Allergens or foods we avoid at home
Day I'll shop

Sources: USDA FSIS, "Leftovers and Food Safety" and "Safe Minimum Internal Temperature Chart" (fsis.usda.gov). US FDA, "Food Allergies: What You Need to Know" (fda.gov), which lists the 9 major food allergens, sesame included since January 1, 2023.

Part 1

The System

Chapter 1

Why Meal Prep Gets Old by Wednesday

"…the same ingredients precooked and rearranged in slightly different ways got really old really fast."

— reader review

Meal prep often doesn't fail at the stove. It fails on Wednesday, with you standing at the fridge, looking at box three of the same chicken and rice.

Here's what usually happens. You cook one protein with one seasoning. You make one carb. You steam one vegetable. You split it all into five boxes. Monday is fine. Tuesday is fine. By Wednesday your mouth knows exactly what's coming, and you order a burrito instead. Thursday's box goes in the trash on Saturday.

Moving the same food around the plate doesn't fix it. If the chicken tastes like lemon and garlic on Monday, it tastes like lemon and garlic on Thursday, whether it sits on rice or in a wrap.

What changes a meal the most is the flavor you add at the end: the sauce, the acid, the spice. So this book cooks the big parts plain-ish and puts the flavor in the sauces.

The plate formula

Every lunch and dinner in this book is built the same way:

1 protein + 1 base + 1 vegetable + 1 sauce

Slot What it does Examples in this book How it's cooked
Protein Fills you up and carries most of the protein Lemon-garlic chicken thighs, turkey taco crumble, baked salmon, crispy tofu Seasoned lightly so it fits several sauces
Base Energy and bulk Oven rice, quinoa, roasted sweet potato, pasta, tortillas Cooked to reheat well (Chapter 13)
Vegetable Color, crunch, fiber Charred broccoli, fajita peppers, slaw, cucumber-tomato salad Roasted, or raw and added on the day
Sauce Decides what the plate tastes like Chipotle-lime yogurt, lighter teriyaki, lemon-tahini Made cold in 5 minutes, stored apart

The protein, base and vegetable are your batch. The sauce is your steering wheel.

The four flavor families

The 12 sauces are grouped into four flavor families, three sauces each. Each family has proteins, bases and vegetables that fit it.

Family Code Tastes like Sauces Fits well with
Mexican-inspired MEX Lime, chipotle, cumin, cilantro Chipotle-Lime Yogurt · Tomatillo Salsa Verde · Quick Red Enchilada Sauce Rice, tortillas, sweet potato, fajita peppers
Asian-inspired ASI Soy, ginger, sesame, chili Lighter Teriyaki · Peanut-Ginger · Gochujang-Honey Rice, quinoa, pasta, broccoli, slaw, green beans
Mediterranean MED Lemon, garlic, herbs, yogurt Tzatziki · Lemon-Tahini · Chimichurri Quinoa, rice, potatoes, cucumber-tomato salad
American comfort COM Smoky, sweet-tangy, creamy Smoky BBQ · Honey-Mustard Yogurt · Creamy Tomato-Garlic Potatoes, sweet potato, pasta, green beans

Some proteins fit every family. The lemon-garlic chicken thighs, the baked chicken breast and the salmon are seasoned so any sauce works on them. Others fit two or three families. The turkey taco crumble is MEX only. Each recipe card tells you which.

One tray, three different plates

This is how Week 1 uses one tray of lemon-garlic chicken thighs with three sauces:

Day Protein Base Vegetable Sauce Family
Plate 1 Lemon-garlic chicken Oven rice Fajita peppers Chipotle-lime yogurt MEX
Plate 2 Lemon-garlic chicken Oven rice Charred broccoli Lighter teriyaki ASI
Plate 3 Lemon-garlic chicken Roasted sweet potato Charred broccoli Lemon-tahini MED

Same chicken. Same Sunday. Three meals that don't taste alike. Add a second protein (the turkey crumble) and Week 1 gets to five different plates from two proteins, two bases, two vegetables and three sauces.

That's the whole trick. You cook a small number of parts and change the combination.

Two rules that keep the week interesting

Rule 1: no sauce two days in a row. If Monday is chipotle-lime, Tuesday is something else. This one rule does most of the work.

Rule 2: something crunchy at least twice a week. Roasted food softens in the fridge. A raw slaw or a cucumber-tomato salad added on the day gives the box a different texture. Chapter 4 shows how to store those parts apart.

What "48 plates" and "Mix & Match" mean

Chapter 16 has 48 signature plates: combinations worked out in full, with calories, protein, allergens and cost for the assembled plate. Twelve per flavor family.

It also has a Mix & Match chart. It shows which parts go together by flavor family; 1,815 combinations follow the rules above. Those combinations were not each cooked one by one. They're a map of what should go together, built from the rules. You'll use it once you're ready to plan your own weeks (Week 4 walks you through it).

Your action

The 5-box test

Plan any five boxes, on paper, before you cook anything. Use the parts from the tables above or the ones you already like to cook.

Box Protein Base Vegetable Sauce Family
Mon
Tue
Wed
Thu
Fri

Now check it:

  • No sauce appears two days in a row.
  • At least two boxes have something raw or crunchy.
  • You use no more than 2 proteins, 2 bases and 2 vegetables in total. (More than that and Sunday gets long.)
  • At least two different flavor families.

If a box fails a check, change the sauce first. It's the cheapest fix and it changes the most.

Chapter 2

Your Sunday, One Sheet: How to Read It

"…this was more frustration than I wanted to put into a day off."

— reader review

Sunday is your day off. If meal prep eats all of it, you'll stop doing it.

Most lost Sundays look alike. You read recipe one and start it. Then you read recipe two and find out it needed the oven at a different temperature. The oven sits empty while you chop. Three things finish at the same minute, or one thing finishes 40 minutes after everything else. You don't know how much goes in each box, so the last box is half empty.

None of that is about cooking skill. It's about order.

The Sunday Sheet fixes the order. It takes every recipe for the week and merges them into one timeline on one page. You read the sheet, not the recipes.

What's on the sheet, top to bottom

1. The header. Week number. Cooking for 1 or 2. Number of boxes. Oven temperature. Estimated time, which is about 2 hours for one person's week. Plus an empty box where you write your real time.

2. Before minute 0. About 10 minutes of setup. Clear the counter. Empty the dishwasher or the drying rack. Line the sheet pans. Take out the scale, the thermometer and every container you'll fill. Set the containers on the table with their lids off.

3. The timeline. Four blocks, always in the same order:

Order Block Why it goes here
1 OVEN It takes longest and needs you least. Start it first and it works while you do the rest.
2 STOVE It needs you at the pan, in short bursts. It fits in while the oven runs.
3 COLD Sauces, slaw, cutting raw vegetables. No heat. Fills the gaps while hot food cooks and cools.
4 ASSEMBLY Weighing into boxes, labeling, into the fridge or freezer. Last, when everything is done.

The blocks overlap. The oven block starts first, but the stove and cold work happen while it's still running. The sheet marks those tasks with ⏱ while the oven runs.

4. The assembly table. How many grams of each part go in each box, for 1 or 2 people. Which boxes go to the fridge. Which go to the freezer (the day-5 rule, Chapter 4).

How to read one row

Each line of the timeline has five columns:

Clock Station Do this Done when Amount
0:00 OVEN Heat oven to 425 °F / 220 °C. Line 2 sheet pans with parchment. Oven signals it's hot —
0:05 PREP ⏱ Cut sweet potatoes into ¾-inch (2 cm) cubes. Cubes about the same size see list
0:15 OVEN Chicken and sweet potatoes in, on separate pans. Set a timer for each pan. — —
0:20 STOVE ⏱ Brown the ground turkey in a large skillet, breaking it up. No pink left. 165 °F / 74 °C see list
0:35 COLD ⏱ Stir the chipotle-lime yogurt sauce. Spoon into small cups. Smooth, no lumps 50 g per cup
… ASSEMBLY Weigh each part into the boxes. Sauce stays in its cup. Every box closed and labeled see assembly table

These rows show the layout. The real minutes and amounts for each week are in Chapters 8 to 11.

Clock is minutes from when you start, not time of day. If you start at 10:00, 0:35 means 10:35.

Done when is the most important column. Ovens differ, pans differ, cuts of meat differ. A timer tells you when to look. The done signal tells you when to stop. For meat, the signal is a temperature, read with a thermometer in the thickest part.

Safe internal temperatures

These are the USDA FSIS minimums. They're printed on every sheet where meat or fish cooks.

Food Safe minimum
Chicken and turkey: whole pieces and ground 165 °F / 74 °C
Ground beef and ground pork 160 °F / 71 °C
Pork chops, roasts, shoulder 145 °F / 63 °C, then rest 3 minutes
Fish, including salmon 145 °F / 63 °C, or flesh flakes easily and is opaque
Egg dishes (egg bites, casseroles) 160 °F / 71 °C

Ground turkey trips people up. It's poultry, so it's 165 °F, not 160 °F.

Pulled pork is different again. It's safe at 145 °F, but it only pulls apart when you cook it much longer, so its recipe gives a higher target for texture.

Five habits that save time

  1. Read the whole sheet the night before. It takes 5 minutes. You'll spot what needs thawing or what you forgot to buy.
  2. One timer per pan, with a name. Phone timers can be labeled: "chicken", "rice". Three unlabeled beeps in a row is how food burns.
  3. Tick each row as you finish it. Use a pencil. If you get interrupted, you know where you were.
  4. Wash as you wait. Each ⏱ gap longer than 5 minutes with nothing listed is a good time for the cutting board and bowls.
  5. Don't start a new block early because you're bored. The order is there so the oven and the fridge aren't fighting each other.

About "about 2 hours"

The plan weeks are built to fit a cooking session of about 2 hours for one person. That's a target, not a stopwatch result you're guaranteed to hit. Your first Sunday will likely take longer. Cooking for two adds mostly cutting and portioning time.

The oven time stays close to the same, as long as the food still fits in a single layer on the pans. If pieces overlap, they steam instead of browning. Use another pan.

Your action

The dry run

The night before your first Sunday:

  • Read the sheet from top to bottom once.
  • Check you own every pan on the setup list (Chapter 7).
  • Count your containers. You need one per box, plus the sauce cups.
  • Check the fridge has room for 10 boxes. Make room now, not with hot food in your hands.
  • Write your start time here: ______

When you finish on Sunday:

Real finish time
Total minutes
The step that took longest
One thing to change next week

Source: USDA FSIS, "Safe Minimum Internal Temperature Chart" (fsis.usda.gov).

Chapter 3

Portions That Add Up

"recipe portions are inconsistent. Sometimes we got a scant 4 servings, sometimes 6, sometimes more."

— reader review

"Serves 4" is a guess. It depends on whose plate, how hungry, and how big the potatoes were. When you cook for a week, a wrong guess shows up on Thursday as one box with half the chicken.

This book doesn't use "serves". It uses grams and boxes.

What every recipe tells you

Each recipe card starts with one line like this:

Makes: 5 portions · about 700 g cooked in total · about 140 g per portion

That line answers two questions before you start: how much food comes out, and how many boxes it fills.

The calories and protein on the card are for one of those portions. That means one fifth of the batch if it makes 5, one tenth if it makes 10.

The batch-divide method

Cooked weight is never exact. Chicken loses water in the oven. Rice soaks it up. Ten minutes more or less changes the number.

So don't trust the raw weight to tell you the box weight. Weigh what came out.

  1. Put a bowl on your kitchen scale and press tare so it reads 0.
  2. Tip the whole cooked batch into the bowl. Write down the weight.
  3. Divide by the number of boxes. That's your grams per box.
  4. Put an empty box on the scale, tare, and fill it to that number. Repeat.

Example: the chicken comes out at 690 g and you need 5 boxes. 690 ÷ 5 = 138 g per box.

Every box ends up with the same share of the batch. And the calories and protein per portion on the card stay right, because each box really is one fifth.

Weighing 10 boxes takes a few minutes once the scale is out. It's the step most people skip and the one that stops the half-empty Thursday box.

The reference portion

When you build your own plates (Path B), start from this box. It's the size used across the 48 signature plates. You can scale it up or down, but change one slot at a time.

Slot Per box (cooked) Roughly from (raw or dry) Kitchen picture
Protein 130-140 g (about 4½-5 oz) 180 g raw boneless meat A deck of cards, a bit thicker
Base 150-170 g cooked rice, quinoa or pasta 60 g dry rice or quinoa, 70 g dry pasta About 1 cup cooked
Base, potato 170-200 g roasted about 200 g raw A fist and a half
Vegetable 100 g roasted, or 80-100 g raw 120-140 g raw before roasting A packed cup
Sauce 40-50 g — About 3 tablespoons

The raw column is a rough guide for shopping. The cooked column is what you weigh. If they disagree, the scale wins.

×1 or ×2: how many boxes

Every shopping list and every Sunday Sheet has two columns.

Column Boxes Covers Breakfasts
×1 10 One person: lunch + dinner, Monday to Friday. Or two people: lunch only. 5
×2 20 Two people: lunch + dinner, Monday to Friday. 10

The amounts in the ×2 column are worked out for you. Two things don't double:

  • Cooking time. A pan with twice the food doesn't need twice the minutes. It needs the same done signal. Use more pans, not more time.
  • Pan space. Food has to sit in one layer to roast. If it doesn't fit, use a third pan or cook a second round. The ×2 sheets note where that happens.

How the boxes split across the week

With the ×1 column, 10 boxes come out on Sunday. They don't all go in the fridge.

Mon Tue Wed Thu Fri
Boxes 2 2 2 2 2
Stored in fridge fridge fridge fridge freezer

The two Friday boxes go to the freezer on Sunday. Chapter 4 explains why, and when to move them back.

Chapter 4

Will It Last Until Thursday? Storage, Freezing, Reheating

"…the taste of the food we cooked ended up being so bad that we ended up throwing away food every single week…"

— reader review

Two things send prepped food to the trash. You're not sure it's still safe. Or it's safe but tastes tired. This chapter handles both.

Among the top threads of the year in a large meal prep forum was one titled: "is it fine to store my food like this for four-five days?" Four is fine. Five is where the answer changes.

The day-5 rule

USDA FSIS says cooked leftovers keep 3 to 4 days in the fridge, at 40 °F (4 °C) or colder.

Count from Sunday:

Sun Mon Tue Wed Thu Fri
cook (day 0) day 1 day 2 day 3 day 4 day 5

Thursday is day 4. Friday is day 5, outside the window.

So the rule in this book is:

Anything you'll eat on day 5 goes in the freezer on Sunday.

Then on Thursday morning, move Friday's boxes from the freezer to the fridge. They thaw during the day and they're ready for Friday. Every Sunday Sheet ends with this step, and the printable fridge card has a Thursday reminder.

Two more rules:

  • Salmon is eaten on day 1 or 2. It's safe for the same 3-4 days, but cooked fish dries out and smells stronger as days pass. The plan puts it at the start of the week.
  • Freeze on Sunday, not on Wednesday. Freezing doesn't reset the clock. A box that has already spent three days in the fridge comes out of the freezer as a day-3 box.

Cool it fast

Bacteria grow fastest between 40 °F and 140 °F (4-60 °C). FSIS calls it the Danger Zone. The rule is to get cooked food into the fridge within 2 hours of cooking. If the room is above 90 °F (32 °C), within 1 hour.

You don't have to wait for food to cool before it goes in the fridge. FSIS says hot food can go straight in. What matters is that it cools quickly, and that means shallow.

  • Spread it out. A deep pot of chili cools slowly in the middle. Split it into shallow containers, about 2 inches (5 cm) deep or less.
  • Leave space. Don't stack ten hot boxes in a tight block. Leave gaps so cold air moves.
  • Lids on loosely for the first 20-30 minutes, then close them.

Rice needs extra care

Rice can carry spores of a bacterium called Bacillus cereus. Cooking doesn't kill the spores. If cooked rice sits warm on the counter for hours, they can grow and make a toxin that reheating won't destroy.

So, as soon as the rice is done:

  1. Spread it in a thin layer on a sheet pan or in wide, shallow containers.
  2. Let the steam escape for about 10 minutes.
  3. Into the fridge. All of it within 2 hours of coming off the heat.

The same goes for quinoa and pasta. Cooling rice fast also keeps the grains apart, so it reheats better.

Freezer basics

  • Set the freezer at 0 °F (-18 °C). At that temperature, food stays safe for as long as it stays frozen.
  • Quality is a different clock. Most cooked dishes taste best if eaten within 2-3 months (soups, stews, casseroles) and cooked meat within about 2-6 months. Each recipe card gives its own number. Write the date on every box.
  • Thaw in the fridge. A single box needs about a day. Never thaw on the counter.
  • If you forget: thaw in the microwave on defrost, then reheat and eat right away.

Reheat to 165 °F

FSIS says leftovers should be reheated to 165 °F (74 °C). Microwaves heat unevenly, so:

  • Cover the box loosely (a lid set on top, not snapped shut).
  • Heat, stir or turn the food, heat again.
  • Check the thickest piece. Steaming all the way through is the visual sign. A thermometer is the sure one.

Keep the taste: store parts apart

Food tastes tired by Thursday for reasons you can control.

Part What goes wrong What to do
Sauces and dressings They soak into rice and pasta, which turn soggy Separate small cup. Add after reheating.
Yogurt sauces (tzatziki, chipotle-lime) They split and turn grainy if heated Never heat. Add cold, on top of the hot food.
Raw crunch (slaw, cucumber-tomato salad) Goes limp, gets watery Slaw: own container, keeps its crunch a few days. Cucumber-tomato: cut on the day, 3 minutes.
Pasta Turns mushy Cook 2 minutes short of the package time, toss with a little oil.
Roasted potatoes Go soft in the microwave Reheat in an air fryer or oven, 5-8 minutes.
Rice Dries out and goes hard Sprinkle 1 tablespoon of water on top, cover, then heat.
Chicken Dries out 1 tablespoon of water or sauce, cover, medium power.

The two-zone box. Many boxes have a hot side and a cold side. Take the sauce cup and any raw parts out. Heat the rest. Put the cold parts back on top. It takes 20 seconds and it's the difference between a Thursday lunch and a sad one.

Wake it up. Flavors fade in the fridge. A squeeze of lime or lemon, a pinch of salt or a few fresh herbs right before you eat bring them back. The recipe cards suggest one for each plate.

To print: the fridge cards (day-5 rule, reheating by part) are pages 20-21 of the printable pack.
Your action

  • Friday's boxes are in the freezer.
  • Salmon boxes are labeled Mon or Tue.
  • Sauce cups are in the fridge door or next to their boxes.
  • Phone reminder set for Thursday morning: move Friday's boxes to the fridge.

Sources: USDA FSIS (fsis.usda.gov), "Leftovers and Food Safety", "Refrigeration and Food Safety", "Freezing and Food Safety", "The Big Thaw: Safe Defrosting Methods" and "Danger Zone (40 °F-140 °F)". US FDA, Bad Bug Book, 2nd ed., chapter on Bacillus cereus (fda.gov).

Chapter 5

What a Plate Really Costs

"This book calls for 4-5 lbs of meat minimum, each week."

— reader review

Meat is the biggest line on almost any meal prep receipt. A plan that quietly asks for 4 or 5 pounds of it every week gets expensive fast, and you only notice at the register.

This chapter gives you a way to know the cost before you shop. It also tells you how much meat each week of the plan uses, so nothing is hidden.

Prices change. Read our numbers that way.

Food prices move every week. They differ by store, by city and by brand. Any price printed in a book is old by the time you read it.

So every cost in this book comes with a store type, a region and a date. Treat it as a reference point, not a quote. Your receipt is the real number. The method below lets you work out your own in a few minutes.

The table below uses the recipes of each weekly sheet. "One batch" is the full shopping for that week as written in the recipes; "double batch" is the same plan cooked twice. A plate is one protein + one base + one vegetable + one sauce, one portion each.

Week Plates (avg. per plate) One batch Double batch (x2)
1 $3.65 $43.70 $87.45
2 $3.65 $47.15 $94.30
3 $4.85 $71.35 $142.70
4 $3.25 $39.65 $79.25

Across all 48 signature plates the average is about $3.75 per plate (cheapest $1.80, priciest $6.65).

US average retail prices, USDA ERS (meat, eggs, dairy: Sep 2026; fruit and vegetables: 2023 data); your store will differ. Pantry items, spices and sauces without a USDA figure are our own reference estimates (about 36% of the total comes from USDA data). Rounded to the nearest 5 cents; no yield loss from trimming is applied.

The method: cost per portion

Cost per portion = price you paid for the package ÷ number of portions you get from it

That's it for anything you use up in one week: meat, a bag of broccoli, a tub of yogurt.

For things you use a little of at a time (oil, spices, soy sauce), work out the share:

Cost of what you used = package price ÷ package size × amount used

Example with made-up numbers, only to show the math:

Item Package price Gives Cost per portion
Pack of chicken thighs $10.00 5 portions $10.00 ÷ 5 = $2.00
Bag of rice, 2 lb (907 g) $3.00 about 15 portions of 60 g $3.00 ÷ 15 = $0.20
Olive oil: 7 g from a 500 g bottle $8.00 — $8.00 ÷ 500 × 7 = $0.11

These are not real prices. They're round numbers so the steps are easy to follow.

Add up the parts of one plate and you have the plate's cost. Add up the shopping list, plus the share of pantry items used, and you have the week.

Read the shelf tag, not the sticker

US grocery shelf tags usually show a unit price next to the price: per pound, per ounce, per count. It's smaller print, often on the left. That's the number to compare.

The big pack isn't always cheaper per pound. The store brand often is. Compare unit prices, not package prices.

How much meat the plan uses

Raw meat and fish per week, from each week's shopping list:

Week Main proteins Raw meat or fish, ×1
1 · Starter Chicken thighs, ground turkey 4 lb (1.8 kg)
2 · Slow-cooker Beef stretched with lentils, slow-cooker chicken about 3.2 lb (1.45 kg)
3 · Freezer-ahead Turkey meatballs ×2, salmon 6 lb (2.7 kg), of which 2 lb of turkey becomes a 4-portion freezer reserve
4 · Mix-it-yourself Tofu, pulled pork about 3.5 lb (1.6 kg), plus 2 lb tofu

Week 1 keeps things familiar for your first Sunday. Week 3 is the biggest shop because a third of it goes to the freezer for later. If that's too much for your budget, start with Week 4 instead: at average US prices it's the cheapest of the four. It uses the same sheet layout.

Five ways the plan keeps cost down

  1. Stretch ground meat with lentils. The Beef & Lentil Power Mince replaces part of the beef with cooked lentils. You get more portions per pound of beef, plus fiber, and the sauce carries the flavor.
  2. Thighs before breasts. Boneless thighs are often cheaper per pound and stay juicier when reheated. Check the unit price at your store.
  3. Use whole packages. The recipes aim to use standard package sizes, and each shopping list notes leftovers you can use the next week. A half-used pack of meat at the back of the fridge is money in the trash.
  4. Frozen vegetables are fine. Frozen broccoli or green beans cost the same in January as in June and never wilt in the drawer. They roast well if you spread them out and give them a few extra minutes.
  5. Buy the pantry once. Spices and sauces are a big cost the first week and almost nothing after. Chapter 7 lists what to keep, so the weekly list stays short.

What the plan skips: precut vegetables. They save time, and they often cost noticeably more per pound. Cutting is built into the Sunday Sheet, during oven time.

Your action

The cost sheet

Keep your receipt from your first shop. Fill this in on Sunday night. It takes about 10 minutes.

Chapter 6

Protein You Can Count

"The recipes don't feel overly 'healthy' in a bad way. they just taste like normal food"

— reader review

High-protein food has a reputation: dry chicken, plain rice, no sauce, eaten because you have to. That's not the food in this book. These are normal plates, the kind you'd order, with the numbers worked out so you don't have to guess.

This chapter shows where those numbers come from, how to read them, and how to add them up when you build your own plate.

Where the numbers come from

Every calorie and protein figure in this book is calculated, not estimated.

  1. The data. USDA FoodData Central, the SR Legacy dataset. It's the US Department of Agriculture's public food composition database.
  2. The weights. Each ingredient is entered in grams, raw or dry, as you buy it: raw chicken, dry rice, raw broccoli. It's matched to the USDA entry in the same state ("chicken thigh, meat only, raw", not "roasted").
  3. Everything counts. Oil, sauce, honey, cheese. If it goes in the pan, it's in the total.
  4. Divide. The batch total is divided by the number of portions on the card.
  5. Round. Calories to the nearest 10. Protein to the nearest gram.

Why raw weights? Raw is what you buy and can weigh before cooking. Cooked weight changes with oven time, because cooking mostly drives off water. The protein stays in the pan. So a total built from raw weights stays the same however long the chicken roasted. That's also why Chapter 3 tells you to divide the cooked batch evenly: each box then carries its share of the raw total.

What this can't do.

  • Some fat melts off. With fattier cuts (thighs, pork shoulder), part of the fat stays in the pan. The real calories in your box can be a little lower than the card.
  • Your brand isn't the USDA average. For packaged foods (yogurt, tortillas, sauces, protein-added products), the label on your package is more accurate than our figure. If they differ a lot, use the label.

How to read the line on a card

Every recipe and plate shows a line like this:

Per portion: 560 kcal · 48 g protein · 56 g carbs · 15 g fat · USDA FoodData Central

  • Per portion means one box, one fifth of a 5-portion batch, split as in Chapter 3.
  • kcal is food energy, what US labels call Calories.
  • Protein, carbs, fat are in grams.

These numbers describe the food. They don't say how much of it you should eat. That depends on you, and it's a conversation for a registered dietitian or your doctor (see Start Here).

One plate, worked out

Here's a simplified plate, calculated the same way as every recipe. It isn't a recipe card. The sauce is only its yogurt base, so the steps are easy to follow.

Part Raw or dry, per portion kcal Protein
Chicken thigh, boneless, skinless 180 g raw 220 35 g
White rice, long-grain 60 g dry 220 4 g
Broccoli + olive oil 100 g raw + 7 g oil 100 3 g
Plain nonfat Greek yogurt (sauce base) 50 g 30 5 g
Plate, calculated as a whole 560 48 g

Calculated with USDA FoodData Central SR Legacy. Carbs 56 g, fat 15 g, fiber 3 g.

The rows add up to 570 kcal and 47 g, not 560 and 48. That's rounding: each row is rounded on its own. The bottom line is calculated from the unrounded numbers, so it's the one to trust.

Swap the protein, see the real number

Now replace the chicken with 150 g of raw firm tofu. Same rice, broccoli, oil and yogurt.

Plate kcal Protein
With chicken thigh (180 g raw) 560 48 g
With firm tofu (150 g raw) 560 38 g

Plant-based plates in this book usually land lower in protein than meat plates of the same size. We print the real figure for each one. We don't average it away or round it up.

If you want more protein on a plant plate, the usual moves are a bigger protein portion, a yogurt- or tofu-based sauce, or edamame on the side. Each changes the calories too, so recalculate with the sheet below.

Allergens on a combined plate

Each component card lists its allergens, from the 9 major food allergens recognized in the US: milk, eggs, fish, crustacean shellfish, tree nuts, peanuts, wheat, soybeans, sesame.

When you combine parts, the plate has every allergen of every part. A plain chicken thigh has none. Add rice: still none. Add teriyaki made with regular soy sauce: now it has soy and wheat.

Common ones to watch in this book:

Ingredient Usually contains Note
Soy sauce Soy, wheat Tamari is often wheat-free. Check the label.
Gochujang Soy, often wheat Varies by brand
Tahini, sesame oil Sesame
Peanut-ginger sauce Peanuts, soy
Tzatziki, yogurt sauces, feta Milk
Tortillas, pasta Wheat
Egg bites Eggs, milk

Use it any time you put together a plate that isn't one of the 48. Copy each part's per-portion line from its card.

The total will be off by up to about 10 kcal and 1-2 g from rounding. That's normal.

Source: U.S. Department of Agriculture, Agricultural Research Service. FoodData Central, SR Legacy (April 2018), fdc.nal.usda.gov. Public domain. Allergen list: US FDA, "Food Allergies: What You Need to Know" (fda.gov).

Chapter 7

Your Pantry & Gear on One Page

"I love that the first few pages tell you all the essential spices, sauces, equipment, etc that you need to have."

— reader review

If every recipe sends you out for a new jar, your weekly list gets long and your cupboard fills with half-used bottles.

The 60 recipes in this book share a small pantry. Buy it once. After that, the weekly shopping list is mostly fresh food: meat, vegetables, yogurt, herbs, lemons and limes.

The pantry: 26 items

The family codes show which flavor family uses each item (Chapter 1). If you only plan to cook one or two families at first, buy only those rows.

Store them well. Opened chipotles in adobo keep longer if you freeze the rest flat in a zip-top bag and break off a piece when needed. Natural peanut butter and tahini separate on the shelf. Stir them well once, then keep them in the fridge if the label says so.

On the weekly list, not in the pantry

These spoil or get used up fast, so they're on each week's shopping list: garlic, fresh ginger, lemons, limes, cilantro, parsley, plain Greek yogurt, eggs, tortillas, pasta, potatoes, sweet potatoes and all meat, fish and tofu.

The gear: 6 things

You probably own most of them.

✓ Tool Why you need it
2 rimmed sheet pans (half-sheet, about 18 × 13 in / 46 × 33 cm) Two trays roast at the same time. Rims stop juices from dripping.
9 × 13 in (23 × 33 cm) baking dish Oven rice and bakes.
Large skillet, about 12 in (30 cm) Ground meat and sauces. Big enough not to crowd.
Pot with a lid, 4 qt (4 L) or more Quinoa, pasta, lentils, soups.
Digital kitchen scale with a tare button, in grams Equal boxes (Chapter 3).
Instant-read thermometer Safe temperatures (Chapters 2 and 4). The one tool you shouldn't skip.

Plus containers. 10 meal containers and 5 small sauce cups for the ×1 week. Double for ×2. Check the bottom or the box: they should say microwave-safe and freezer-safe. Two-compartment boxes help keep sauce and raw parts apart.

Small stuff: parchment paper, foil, masking tape and a marker for labels, a fridge thermometer.

Nice to have, not needed: a slow cooker (Week 2 uses one; the recipe gives an oven option), an air fryer (best for reheating potatoes and tofu), a sharp chef's knife if yours is dull.

One rule that keeps the pantry stocked

When you open the last one, write it on next week's list. Not when it runs out. When you open it. A taped note on the inside of the cupboard door works.

Chapter 8

Week 1 · The Starter Week

"I loved not having to figure out what to eat."

— reader review

This is the week for your first Sunday. Every decision is made. You shop from the list, cook from the sheet and fill the boxes.

Who it's for: you're new to batch cooking, or you tried it and got lost in five recipes at once. It uses one skillet, one small saucepan and the oven. No slow cooker, no blender.

What comes out: 10 boxes (lunch and dinner Monday to Friday for one person, or lunch for two) and 5 breakfasts.

Raw meat this week: 4 lb (1.8 kg) for one person: 2 lb chicken thighs and 2 lb ground turkey. That's the most of any week except Week 3. Week 2 drops to about 3.2 lb.

The week at a glance

Proteins P1 Lemon-Garlic Sheet-Pan Chicken Thighs · P4 Turkey Taco Crumble
Bases B1 Hands-Off Oven Rice · B4 Roasted Sweet Potato Cubes
Vegetables V1 Charred Roasted Broccoli · V2 Sheet-Pan Fajita Peppers & Onions
Sauces S1 Chipotle-Lime Yogurt · S4 Lighter Teriyaki · S8 Lemon-Tahini
Breakfast D1 Spinach-Feta Egg Bites
Oven 450 °F → 425 °F → 350 °F (one oven, two racks)
Stove 1 large skillet, 1 small saucepan
Session About 2 hours, plus 10 minutes of setup. Time yours.
Freezer Friday's 2 boxes go in on Sunday

The Sunday Sheet · Week 1

Before 0:00 (about 10 minutes): clear the counter. Line 4 sheet pans with parchment. Grease a 12-cup muffin tin plus 3 cups of a second tin. Get out the scale, thermometer, an 8×8-inch (20 cm) baking dish with foil, 10 boxes, 6 small sauce cups and 5 breakfast containers. Fill the kettle.

Clock Station Do this Done when Recipe
0:00 OVEN Heat to 450 °F / 230 °C. Oven signals hot —
0:00 PREP ⏱ Cut broccoli into florets with a flat side. Dry well. Toss with oil, salt, garlic powder on pan 1. Florets dry to the touch V1
0:05 PREP ⏱ Whisk the chicken marinade. Toss the thighs in it. Into the fridge. Every piece coated P1
0:10 PREP ⏱ Peel and cut sweet potatoes in ¾-inch (2 cm) cubes. Toss with oil and spices on pan 2. Slice peppers and onions ½ inch (1 cm). Toss with oil and spices on pan 3. Pieces the same size, one layer B4, V2
0:15 OVEN Broccoli in (top rack), cut sides down. Timer: 18 min. — V1
0:15 STOVE ⏱ Skillet, medium heat: onion 4-5 min, spices 30 sec, turkey 7-9 min, then tomato paste + ½ cup water 3-4 min. No pink. 165 °F / 74 °C. Glossy, not soupy P4
0:35 OVEN Broccoli out (18-22 min). Turn oven to 425 °F / 220 °C, door open 1 minute. Tips blackened in spots, stems pierce easily V1
0:35 OVEN Chicken (pan 4) and sweet potatoes (pan 2) in. Timers: chicken 22 min, sweet potato flip at 15 min. — P1, B4
0:35 STOVE ⏱ Turkey off the heat. Spread in a wide dish to cool. Kettle on: 2¼ cups water. Rinse the rice. — P4, B1
0:40 STOVE ⏱ Saucepan: teriyaki. Simmer, whisk in the cornstarch, 1-2 min more. Off heat, sesame oil. Glossy, coats a spoon S4
0:50 OVEN Flip the sweet potatoes. — B4
0:50 COLD ⏱ Stir the chipotle-lime yogurt. Whisk the lemon-tahini. Into the fridge. Even pale orange · thick ribbon S1, S8
0:57-1:03 OVEN Chicken out when the thermometer says so. Rest 5 min on the pan. 165 °F / 74 °C in the thickest piece. Edges browned P1
1:00 OVEN Peppers & onions (pan 3) in. Timer: stir at 15 min. — V2
1:05 OVEN Sweet potatoes out (12-15 min after the flip). Rice in: rice, oil, salt and boiling water in the dish, foil crimped tight. Timer: 25 min. Browned edges, fork slides in B4, B1
1:05 PREP ⏱ Slice the chicken. Wilt the spinach in the skillet 1-2 min, press out water, chop. Whisk eggs + cottage cheese + seasoning. Fill 15 muffin cups with spinach, feta, then egg to ¾. Spinach bright green. Cups ¾ full P1, D1
1:15 OVEN Stir the peppers. — V2
1:25-1:28 OVEN Peppers out (25-28 min). Lime juice on top. Onions soft and golden at the edges V2
1:30-1:35 OVEN Rice out (check at 25 min). Leave covered 10 min. Water gone, small steam holes B1
1:35 OVEN Oven to 350 °F / 175 °C, door open 2-3 min. Egg bites in. Timer: 20 min. — D1
1:45 ASSEMBLY ⏱ Fluff the rice and spread it thin on a pan. Into the fridge within 1 hour. Weigh each batch, divide by its box count (table below). Fill the boxes. All boxes filled all
1:57-2:01 OVEN Egg bites out (20-24 min). Cool 5 min in the tin, lift out. Centers set, knife comes out clean. 160 °F / 71 °C D1
2:05 ASSEMBLY Lids on. Labels on. Fridge for Mon-Thu, freezer for Friday's 2 boxes and 2 spare teriyaki cups. Egg bites: 3 per container once fully cool. Fridge 40 °F / 4 °C or below all

Write your real time here: start ______ finish ______ total ______ min.

Why this order: the broccoli wants the hottest oven, so it goes first. Everything at 425 °F shares the next 50 minutes. The egg bites need the coolest oven, so they go last while you pack. The recipe cards say B1 cooks at 375 °F; at 425 °F it fits beside another tray if the foil is sealed tight, so check it at 25 minutes.

To print: this Sunday Sheet and the shopping list are in the printable pack (pages 2 and 7), one page each.

The assembly table

Weigh each cooked batch, then divide it by the box count. The grams below are what that gives with the yields on the recipe cards. Your batch may weigh a little more or less. The scale wins.

Part Boxes it fills Per box (×1) Goes into
P1 chicken 6 about 113 g (4 oz) M1, A1, D-8
P4 turkey 4 about 175 g (6 oz) M3, M10
B1 rice 6 about 133 g (4¾ oz) M1, M10, A1
B4 sweet potato 4 about 212 g (7½ oz) M3, D-8
V1 broccoli 6 about 108 g (3¾ oz) M10, A1, D-8
V2 peppers & onions 4 about 162 g (5¾ oz) M1, M3
S1 chipotle-lime (cup) 6 about 35 g (2 Tbsp) M1, M3, M10
S4 teriyaki (cup) 2 + 3 spare 34 g A1
S8 lemon-tahini (cup) 2 + 3 spare 34 g D-8
D1 egg bites 5 containers 3 bites breakfast

×2: make every batch twice and fill 20 boxes with the same grams per box. You'll need more pan space, so expect a second oven round for the 425 °F trays. Time it once and write it on your sheet.

Why some boxes get more than a card portion: this week the chicken is shared by 6 boxes and the turkey by 4, so turkey boxes are bigger and chicken boxes smaller than the standard portion in Chapter 16. The menu below shows the numbers for your actual boxes.

Per day (2 boxes + breakfast): Mon and Wed 1,530 kcal · 112 g protein. Tue and Thu 1,750 kcal · 112 g. Fri 1,320 kcal · 96 g. Week average: about 1,580 kcal and 109 g protein a day from this plan. That's what the boxes hold, not a target. The book doesn't tell you how much to eat (Chapter 0).

All figures calculated from USDA FoodData Central SR Legacy with the gram weights on the recipe cards, for the box sizes above. Kcal rounded to 10, protein to 1 g.

Allergens this week: milk, egg, soy, wheat, sesame. Soy sauce usually contains wheat. Read the labels of what you buy.

The day-5 rule, applied

  • Sunday is day 0. Friday is day 5, outside the 3-4 days USDA FSIS gives cooked leftovers in the fridge.
  • Friday = A1 ×2, frozen on Sunday. Chicken, rice and teriyaki all freeze well. The broccoli is safe frozen but comes out softer (that's why the storage chart says "not recommended" for texture). Re-crisp it 5 minutes in a hot oven or air fryer if you mind.
  • Thursday morning: move both A1 boxes to the fridge. They thaw by Friday.
  • No salmon this week, so no day-1-2 rule.
  • S1 (chipotle-lime yogurt) doesn't freeze, and it's only on Monday-Thursday plates. That's why it isn't on Friday.

Chapter 9

Week 2 · The Slow-Cooker Week

"Simply because it gets expensive and hard to source all the meat sometimes... especially the more expensive cuts."

— reader review

This week spends less on meat without cutting the protein. Two tricks do it. Lentils stretch 1 lb of ground beef into 5 portions. Chicken breast goes in the slow cooker with a jar of salsa and needs nothing from you for hours.

Who it's for: you did Week 1 and want a Sunday where the slow cooker does part of the work, and less meat on the list thanks to lentils.

What comes out: 10 boxes and 5 jars of overnight oats.

Raw meat this week: about 3.2 lb (1.45 kg) for one person: 1 lb ground beef and 2.2 lb chicken breast. That's 0.8 lb less than Week 1.

The week at a glance

Proteins P5 Beef & Lentil Power Mince · P2 Slow-Cooker Salsa Shredded Chicken
Bases B3 Crispy Roasted Baby Potatoes · B6 Wraps & Tortillas (no cooking)
Vegetables V3 Roasted Zucchini & Red Onion · V2 Sheet-Pan Fajita Peppers & Onions
Sauces S3 Quick Red Enchilada · S10 Smoky BBQ (less sugar) · S2 Tomatillo Salsa Verde
Breakfast D3 Greek Yogurt Overnight Oats
Oven 425 °F the whole time, then the broiler
Stove 1 large pot, 2 small saucepans · plus a slow cooker · a blender for the salsa verde
Session About 2 hours at the counter. The slow cooker starts 2½ hours earlier and works alone.
Freezer Friday's 2 boxes go in on Sunday

No slow cooker? The P2 card doesn't have a tested oven method yet [SIN DATO]. Use P3 Never-Dry Baked Chicken Breast instead: same chicken, same weight. It roasts at 425 °F, so it shares the oven with this week's trays. Shred or slice it and stir in 2 Tbsp of jarred salsa per box. The numbers in the menu will change a little.

The Sunday Sheet · Week 2

2½ hours before 0:00 (10 minutes): chicken breasts in the slow cooker in one layer. Cumin, chili powder, garlic powder and salt on top. Pour the salsa over. No water. Lid on, HIGH. Now go do something else.

Before 0:00 (about 10 minutes): line 2 sheet pans with parchment, keep a third pan bare for the potatoes. Get out the scale, thermometer, a large pot, 2 small saucepans, blender, 10 boxes, 10 sauce cups, 5 jars of at least 12 oz (350 ml).

Clock Station Do this Done when Recipe
0:00 OVEN Heat to 425 °F / 220 °C with the bare pan inside. Oven signals hot B3
0:00 STOVE Large pot, medium-high: oil, then the beef. Break it up. 6-8 min. No pink. 160 °F / 71 °C P5
0:00 PREP ⏱ Halve the potatoes (quarter big ones). Dry. Toss with oil and spices. Slice peppers and onions ½ inch. Toss with oil and spices on a lined pan. Same-size pieces B3, V2
0:10 OVEN Potatoes onto the hot pan, cut side down (lower rack). Timer: 20 min, then flip. Peppers in (top rack). Timer: stir at 15 min. They sizzle B3, V2
0:10 STOVE ⏱ Pot: onion + carrot 5 min, garlic + tomato paste + spices 1 min, then rinsed lentils, salt, 3 cups water. Boil, then simmer partly covered 22-28 min. Stir now and then. — P5
0:15 STOVE ⏱ Saucepan 1: whisk all the BBQ ingredients, bring to a gentle bubble, then low 10-12 min. Spoon leaves a clear trail S10
0:25 OVEN Stir the peppers. — V2
0:30 OVEN Flip the potatoes. Timer: 15 min. — B3
0:30 PREP ⏱ Cut zucchini in ½-inch half-moons, red onion in wedges. Toss with oil and spices on a lined pan. — V3
0:35 STOVE ⏱ Saucepan 2: oil, flour + spices 45-60 sec, then tomato sauce, water, vinegar, salt. Low 8-10 min. Coats a spoon, brick red S3
0:38 OVEN Peppers out (25-28 min). Lime on top. Zucchini in. Timer: turn at 12 min. Onions soft and golden at the edges V2, V3
0:45-0:50 OVEN Potatoes out. Deep golden cut sides, knife slides in B3
0:45 STOVE Check the beef and lentils. Add ¼ cup water at a time if it dries too soon. Off the heat when done. Lentils tender, pot thick like chili P5
0:50 SLOW COOKER Check the chicken. Shred with 2 forks. Stir in lime juice and ½ cup of the cooking liquid. If it isn't ready, check again at 1:10. 165 °F / 74 °C, shreds with no effort P2
0:55 COLD ⏱ Overnight oats: dry mix, then yogurt, milk, honey, vanilla. Into 5 jars, berries on top. Fridge. No dry pockets D3
0:58-1:03 OVEN Zucchini out (20-25 min). Lemon juice on top. Turn on the broiler, high. Golden edges, tender middle V3
1:05 OVEN Tomatillos, jalapeño, onion, unpeeled garlic on a foil-lined pan under the broiler, 5-7 min. Flip, 4-5 min more. Blistered, soft, slumped S2
1:05 ASSEMBLY ⏱ Weigh each batch, divide by its box count (table below). Fill the boxes. Stack 6 tortillas with parchment between them, into a zip bag. — all
1:20 COLD Cool the broiled salsa 5 min. Slip the garlic out of its skin. Blend with cilantro, lime, salt. Bright, not flat S2
1:30 ASSEMBLY Sauce into cups. Lids, labels. Fridge for Mon-Thu, freezer for Friday's 2 M11 boxes. The tortilla bag stays in the fridge. Fridge 40 °F / 4 °C or below all

Write your real time here: start ______ finish ______ total ______ min.

Why this order: one oven temperature all session, so nothing waits for the oven to change. The broiler goes last because it needs the whole top of the oven. The lentil pot simmers on its own in the middle of the session.

Chapter 10

Week 3 · The Freezer-Ahead Week

"Keep em in the freezer and you're good for weeks."

— reader review

This week you cook a little more than you eat. The meatballs are made twice. One batch feeds this week. The other goes into the freezer as a reserve for a week when Sunday doesn't happen.

Who it's for: you've done two weeks and you know a busy week is coming. Or you want a stock in the freezer before you need it.

What comes out: 10 boxes, 5 breakfast burritos, 1 salmon dinner for Sunday night, and 4 meatball portions (24 meatballs) for the freezer.

Raw meat and fish this week: 6 lb (2.7 kg) for one person: 4 lb ground turkey and 2 lb salmon. It's the biggest shop of the plan because a third of it is the freezer reserve.

The week at a glance

Proteins P6 Freezer Turkey Meatballs (×2) · P8 Simple Baked Salmon
Bases B5 Reheat-Ready Pasta · B2 Fluffy Quinoa
Vegetables V4 Garlic Green Beans · V3 Roasted Zucchini & Red Onion
Sauces S12 Creamy Tomato-Garlic · S9 Chimichurri · S4 Lighter Teriyaki
Breakfast D5 Freezer Breakfast Burritos
Oven 425 °F → 400 °F
Stove 1 large pot, 1 medium pot with lid, 1 nonstick pan, 2 small saucepans
Session About 2 hours, plus 10 minutes of setup. Time yours.
Freezer Friday's 2 boxes, 1 burrito, 24 meatballs

The Sunday Sheet · Week 3

Before 0:00 (about 10 minutes): line 5 sheet pans with parchment. Fill a large pot with 3 quarts (3 L) of water. Get out the scale, thermometer, 10 boxes, 9 sauce cups, a freezer bag for the meatballs, foil or parchment for 5 burritos.

Clock Station Do this Done when Recipe
0:00 OVEN Heat to 425 °F / 220 °C. Oven signals hot —
0:00 STOVE Pasta water on, high, lid on. — B5
0:00 PREP ⏱ Trim and dry the green beans. Toss with oil, salt, pepper on pan 1. Cut zucchini in ½-inch half-moons, red onion in wedges. Toss on pan 2. One layer, not crowded V4, V3
0:10 OVEN Beans and zucchini in. Timers: beans 10 min, zucchini turn at 12 min. — V4, V3
0:10 STOVE ⏱ Salt the boiling water, add the pasta. Cook 1-2 min less than the package says. Drain, don't rinse, toss with oil, spread on a tray. Thin white dot in the center B5
0:12 STOVE ⏱ Medium pot: rinse quinoa 30 sec, add 3 cups water, oil, salt. Boil, then lowest heat, lid on, 14-16 min. Off heat 5 min, lid on. Water gone, white spiral tails B2
0:15 PREP ⏱ Meatball mix, two batches in one big bowl: egg, crumbs, Parmesan, garlic, spices, parsley, 3 min. Add the turkey, mix just until it comes together. Scoop 60 balls, about 2 Tbsp each, onto pans 3 and 4. Even size, close but not touching P6
0:20 OVEN Beans out, scatter the garlic, back in 4-6 min. Turn the zucchini. — V4, V3
0:26 OVEN Beans out. Lemon juice on top. Wrinkled, brown spots, a little snap. Garlic pale gold V4
0:32-0:35 OVEN Zucchini out (20-25 min). Lemon juice. Turn oven to 400 °F / 205 °C, door open 1-2 min. Golden edges, tender middle V3
0:35 OVEN Both meatball pans in. Timer: 18 min. — P6
0:35 STOVE ⏱ Saucepan 1, medium-low: garlic 30-60 sec, tomato sauce + oregano + salt 8-10 min, then cream cheese on lowest heat, Parmesan off heat. Smooth, creamy pink-orange S12
0:40 STOVE ⏱ Saucepan 2: teriyaki. Simmer, whisk in the cornstarch, 1-2 min. Off heat, sesame oil. Glossy, coats a spoon S4
0:50 PREP ⏱ Pat the salmon dry. Cut into 5 even pieces (about 180 g raw each). On pan 5, skin side down. Oil, lemon, spices. — P8
0:53-0:55 OVEN Meatballs out. Cool 15 min. Browned. 165 °F / 74 °C in the middle P6
0:55 OVEN Salmon in. Timer: 11 min. — P8
0:55 STOVE ⏱ Nonstick pan: onion + red pepper 3-4 min, spices, then the beaten eggs, folding 3-4 min. Onto a plate to cool 10 min. Large soft curds, no wet shine D5
1:06-1:09 OVEN Salmon out. Rest 3 min. Oven off. Flakes with a fork. 145 °F / 63 °C P8
1:10 ASSEMBLY ⏱ Meatballs: 36 into 6 boxes (6 each). The other 24 on a tray into the freezer for 1 hour, then into a bag. Weigh and divide the rest (table below). — all
1:15 PREP Burritos: warm each tortilla 10 sec, add ⅕ of the eggs, 3 Tbsp beans, ⅕ of the cheddar. Fold the sides, roll tight. Wrap each one. 5 tight rolls D5
1:35 COLD Chimichurri, last so it's freshest. Chop parsley and garlic fine, add vinegar, oregano, flakes, salt, then the oil. Sharp and green S9
1:45 ASSEMBLY Sauces into cups. Lids, labels. Fridge for Mon-Thu. Freezer: Friday's A10 + D-5 boxes, 1 burrito, the meatball bag. Salmon portion #5 is Sunday dinner. Fridge 40 °F / 4 °C or below all

Write your real time here: start ______ finish ______ total ______ min.

Why this order: vegetables roast hottest, so they go first. The oven drops 25 degrees for the meatballs and stays there for the salmon. The stove runs the whole session on two burners; the oven never waits for it.

The day-5 rule, applied

  • Salmon on days 1-2. All 4 salmon boxes are Monday and Tuesday. The 5th piece is Sunday dinner. Salmon is safe for 3 days, but it dries and smells stronger as the days pass.
  • Zucchini (V3) keeps 3 days. The zucchini plates are D-3 (Mon-Tue) and C3 (Wed).
  • Friday = A10 + D-5, frozen on Sunday. Meatballs, pasta, quinoa, teriyaki and chimichurri all freeze. The green beans are safe frozen but come out softer.
  • Friday's burrito goes in the freezer too. Burritos keep 4 days in the fridge.
  • Thursday morning: move both Friday boxes and the burrito to the fridge.
  • The reserve: 24 meatballs, labeled with today's date. Best within 3 months. Reheat from frozen: 12 min at 350 °F / 175 °C, until 165 °F / 74 °C in the center. Pair them with any base and sauce from the Mix & Match chart (Chapter 16).

Chapter 11

Week 4 · The Mix-It-Yourself Week

"It would be nice if prep and recipe and serving sizes were all together so you could pick and chose recipes to batch cook together. There is no deviating from the set weeks."

— reader review

Three weeks in, you've followed three sheets. This week is half filled in. Everything is set except one sauce, and you choose it. The point isn't the sauce. It's learning how a choice moves through the sheet, the shopping list and the boxes, so that after this week you can plan your own.

Who it's for: you've done at least one plan week and you want to stop depending on the book.

What comes out: 10 boxes and 5 breakfasts. One protein is tofu, so this week is lighter on meat.

Raw meat this week: about 3.5 lb (1.6 kg) for one person: 3 lb pork shoulder after trimming (buy about 3¾ lb) and 8 oz ground turkey for the egg bites. Plus 2 lb of tofu.

The week at a glance

Proteins P7 Oven Pulled Pork Shoulder · P9 Crispy Baked Tofu
Bases B1 Hands-Off Oven Rice · B2 Fluffy Quinoa
Vegetables V5 Sesame Cabbage-Carrot Slaw (raw) · V1 Charred Roasted Broccoli
Sauces S6 Gochujang-Honey · S5 Peanut-Ginger · your choice (default: S2 Tomatillo Salsa Verde)
Breakfast D2 Turkey & Pepper Egg Bites
Oven 300 °F (pork, before you start) → 450 °F → 425 °F → 350 °F
Stove 1 medium pot with lid, 1 skillet, 1-2 small saucepans
Session About 2 hours at the counter. The pork goes in about 4½ hours earlier and roasts alone.
Freezer Friday's 2 boxes go in on Sunday

Step 1 · Choose your sauce

The sauce goes on M5, the pulled pork + rice + slaw plate. A sauce can go there if it shares a flavor family with all three parts (Chapter 16, Mix & Match). Pulled pork works with Mexican, Asian and Comfort. Rice works with all four. The slaw works with Asian, Mexican and Comfort. So any Mexican, Asian or Comfort sauce fits.

S5 and S6 are already in this week. Pick one of these:

Sauce Family Your M5 box: kcal · protein Adds allergens Sheet slot Freezes?
S2 Tomatillo Salsa Verde (default) Mexican 660 · 51 g none Broiler 12 min + blender, at 1:32 yes, 3 months
S1 Chipotle-Lime Yogurt Mexican 670 · 54 g milk Cold, 5 min, at 1:10 no
S3 Quick Red Enchilada Mexican 670 · 51 g wheat Stove, 15 min, at 1:10 yes, 3 months
S4 Lighter Teriyaki Asian 680 · 51 g soy, wheat, sesame Stove, 12 min, at 1:10 yes, 3 months
S10 Smoky BBQ Comfort 680 · 51 g fish (Worcestershire) Stove, 20 min, at 1:10 yes, 3 months
S11 Honey-Mustard Yogurt Comfort 680 · 53 g milk Cold, 5 min, at 1:10 no
S12 Creamy Tomato-Garlic Comfort 700 · 52 g milk Stove, 15 min, at 1:10 yes, 2 months

The M5 box already contains sesame, soy and wheat (from the slaw dressing). Box numbers calculated from USDA FoodData Central SR Legacy for this week's box sizes.

My sauce this week: ________________ (ID ____ )

Now three things change, and you'll make each change yourself:

  1. The sheet: write your sauce in the row marked ✎ below.
  2. The shopping list: cross out the S2 lines and add your sauce's ingredients (they're on its recipe card, Chapter 15).
  3. The menu: write your M5 numbers from the table above.

The Sunday Sheet · Week 4

About 4½ hours before 0:00 (15 minutes): oven to 300 °F / 150 °C. Trim the pork. Rub it with the sugar and spices. Into a Dutch oven or deep dish with the vinegar and ½ cup water around it. Cover tightly. Roast 4-4½ hours. You're free until 0:00.

Before 0:00 (about 10 minutes): line 2 sheet pans with parchment. Grease 15 muffin cups. Get out an 8×8-inch (20 cm) dish with foil, the scale, thermometer, 10 boxes, 6 dressing cups, 10 sauce cups, 5 breakfast containers. Fill the kettle.

Clock Station Do this Done when Recipe
0:00 OVEN Pork out. Rest 20 min, covered. Oven to 450 °F / 230 °C. 195-205 °F / 90-96 °C, a fork twists in with no resistance P7
0:00 PREP ⏱ Drain the tofu, wrap in towels, press under a heavy pan for 20 min. Cut broccoli into florets, dry, toss with oil, salt, garlic powder on pan 1. Florets dry P9, V1
0:05 STOVE ⏱ Pot: rinse quinoa 30 sec, 3 cups water, oil, salt. Boil, lowest heat, lid on, 14-16 min. Off heat 5 min, lid on. Water gone, white spiral tails B2
0:10 STOVE ⏱ Skillet, medium-high: onion + red pepper 3 min, then turkey, paprika, half the salt 5-6 min. Drain. Cool. No pink. 165 °F / 74 °C D2
0:12 OVEN Broccoli in. Timer: 18 min. — V1
0:20 PREP ⏱ Shred the pork with 2 forks. Skim the fat from the juices. Toss the pork with ½ cup juices. Shreds with no effort P7
0:25 PREP ⏱ Tofu: cut ¾-inch cubes. Soy sauce + oil, then spices, then cornstarch. Spread on pan 2. Kettle on: 2¼ cups. Rinse the rice. Cubes look dry, no white clumps P9, B1
0:30-0:34 OVEN Broccoli out (18-22 min). Oven to 425 °F / 220 °C, door open 1 min. Tips blackened in spots V1
0:34 OVEN Tofu in. Rice in (rice, oil, salt, boiling water, foil crimped tight). Timers: tofu flip at 15 min, rice 25 min. — P9, B1
0:35 STOVE ⏱ Saucepan: gochujang sauce, medium-low 2-3 min, then 1 min more. Off heat, sesame oil. Glossy, slightly thicker S6
0:45 COLD ⏱ Slaw: shred cabbage thin, grate carrots, slice scallions. Whisk the dressing in a jar. Keep them apart. — V5
0:49 OVEN Flip the tofu. Timer: 15 min. — P9
0:55 COLD ⏱ Peanut-ginger: whisk, then warm water 1 Tbsp at a time. Smooth ribbon S5
0:59-1:04 OVEN Rice out (check at 25 min), leave covered 10 min. Tofu out (15-18 min after the flip). Water gone, steam holes · golden, sounds hollow when tapped B1, P9
1:05 OVEN Oven to 350 °F / 175 °C, door open 2-3 min. Whisk eggs + salt + pepper. Turkey mix and cheddar into the cups, egg to ¾. Egg bites in. Timer: 20 min. — D2
1:10 ✎ YOUR SAUCE Cold or stove sauce: make it now. S2: wait for 1:32. Sauce: ____________ (see its card) ____
1:15 ASSEMBLY ⏱ Fluff the rice and spread it thin. Into the fridge within 1 hour. Weigh each batch, divide by its box count (table below). Fill the boxes. — all
1:25-1:30 OVEN Egg bites out (20-24 min). Cool 5 min in the tin, lift out. Set in the middle, knife clean. 160 °F / 71 °C D2
1:32 OVEN Only if S2: broiler on high. Tomatillos, jalapeño, onion, garlic 5-7 min, flip, 4-5 min. Cool 5, blend. Blistered and slumped S2
1:50 ASSEMBLY Sauces and slaw dressing into cups. Lids, labels. Fridge for Mon-Thu, freezer for Friday's A7 + A12. Egg bites: 3 per container once fully cool. Fridge 40 °F / 4 °C or below all

Write your real time here: start ______ finish ______ total ______ min (not counting the pork's 4½ hours alone in the oven).

Why the pork goes in early: at 300 °F it holds the oven for 4-4½ hours, and nothing else in this week cooks at 300 °F. Roasting it before the session frees the oven for the hot trays. Don't roast it on Saturday: that makes Thursday day 5.

To print: this Sunday Sheet and the shopping list are in the printable pack (pages 5 and 10), one page each.

Per day with the default S2 (2 boxes + breakfast): Mon and Wed 1,840 kcal · 130 g protein. Tue 1,800 · 139 g. Thu 1,720 · 126 g. Fri 1,920 · 139 g. Week average: about 1,820 kcal and 133 g protein a day. Other sauces change Tue and Thu by 10-40 kcal. That's what the boxes hold, not a target.

Calculated from USDA FoodData Central SR Legacy for the box sizes above. Kcal rounded to 10, protein to 1 g.

Allergens this week: peanuts, soy, wheat, sesame, egg, milk, plus whatever your sauce adds. Many gochujang brands contain wheat. If anyone eating this week has a peanut allergy, skip S5: use S6 on A2 and A12 instead, and keep the peanut-ginger sauce out of the kitchen that day.

The day-5 rule, applied

  • Friday = A7 + A12, frozen on Sunday. Tofu, pork, rice, quinoa, gochujang sauce and peanut sauce freeze. The broccoli is safe frozen but softer. Re-crisp the tofu 6-8 min at 400 °F / 205 °C after thawing (the microwave makes it soft).
  • The raw slaw doesn't freeze. That's why every slaw plate (A3, M5, A2) is Monday to Thursday. Dress it on the day.
  • If you chose S1 or S11: they don't freeze, and they're fine here because M5 is on Tuesday and Thursday.
  • Thursday morning: move Friday's boxes to the fridge.

Chapter 12

Proteins

"This book calls for 4-5 lbs of meat minimum, each week."

— reader review

Ten proteins, cooked once and used across the week. Six are meat or fish. One (P5) stretches 1 lb of beef with lentils. Three don't need meat at all: tofu (P9), chickpeas (P10) and the egg-based breakfasts in Chapter 17.

How to use this block
  • Pick 2 proteins per week. One from the oven, one from the stove or slow cooker, so they don't fight for space.
  • Check the Families column: it tells you which sauces each protein goes with (Chapter 16).
  • Every card ends with Swap: a cheaper cut, a different meat, or an allergy-friendly version.
  • The chickpeas give 11 g of protein per portion. That's the real number; pair them with a higher-protein breakfast or snack if protein matters to you.
